It's really stressing me out because I just want to do my job right, but I can't seem to control it.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these difficulties with focus and organization since you were a child, or is this a relatively new change in your behavior?\n\nPatient: This is a pretty new change that started about a year ago after I left community college to work full-time at the warehouse. Before this, I was actually really organized and used to plan out woodworking projects and fishing trips down to the last detail. It's incredibly frustrating because this lack of control just isn't like me at all.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any other physical symptoms, such as persistent headaches, vision changes, or episodes where you felt confused or unlike yourself, around the time these behavioral changes began?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't really had any physical issues like headaches or changes in my vision. I was pretty healthy physically before all of this started happening.\n\nDoctor: Besides the confusion and trouble with tasks, have you been feeling persistently sad, hopeless, or unusually worried and anxious for most of the day over this past year?\n\nPatient: I haven't really been feeling persistently sad, hopeless, or unusually anxious. My main issue is just the confusion and not being able to keep my thoughts and actions in order.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your personality, such as becoming more impulsive, socially inappropriate, or less concerned about how your actions affect others compared to how you were before?\n\nPatient: Yes, my family and pastor have pointed out that I seem \"out of step\" and behave in ways that don't fit the situation at home and church. It's more that I just get really mixed up, lose my train of thought, or lose track of what I'm doing in the moment.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been told by a doctor that you have an underlying medical condition, such as a metabolic issue, a hormonal imbalance, or a neurological disorder that could potentially affect your brain function?\n\nPatient: No, I've never been told that I have any underlying medical conditions, metabolic issues, or neurological disorders. I have always been physically healthy and haven't had any diagnoses like that before.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any difficulty with your speech, such as trouble finding the right words or understanding what others are saying, compared to how you were before these changes started?\n\nPatient: Yes, I've had a really hard time keeping my speech and thoughts on track lately. By the end of it, I can see on their faces that they are completely confused and have no idea what I was trying to say.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any involuntary muscle twitches, jerky movements, or changes in your gait and coordination, even if they seem minor?\n\nPatient: I haven't really noticed any involuntary muscle twitches or jerky movements. Sometimes I do find myself holding odd poses, pacing, or making faces without realizing why, but it isn't like an involuntary spasm.\n\nDoctor: When you interact with others, do you find it difficult to follow social rules or restrain yourself from saying or doing things that might be considered rude or embarrassing, even when you know you shouldn't?"

It's really stressing me out because I just want to do my job right, but I can't seem to control it.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these difficulties with focus and organization since you were a child, or is this a relatively new change in your behavior?\n\nPatient: This is a pretty new change that started about a year ago after I left community college to work full-time at the warehouse. Before this, I was actually really organized and used to plan out woodworking projects and fishing trips down to the last detail. It's incredibly frustrating because this lack of control just isn't like me at all.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any other physical symptoms, such as persistent headaches, vision changes, or episodes where you felt confused or unlike yourself, around the time these behavioral changes began?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't really had any physical issues like headaches or changes in my vision. But I definitely feel extremely confused lately, and I just can't seem to keep my actions and thoughts organized anymore.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any significant medical illnesses, head injuries, or new medications that coincided with the start of these behavioral changes a year ago?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't had any head injuries or major medical illnesses, and I wasn't taking any new medications back then. I was pretty healthy physically before all of this started happening.\n\nDoctor: Besides the confusion and trouble with tasks, have you been feeling persistently sad, hopeless, or unusually worried and anxious for most of the day over this past year?\n\nPatient: I haven't really been feeling persistently sad, hopeless, or unusually anxious. While I am stressed and frustrated by the trouble I'm having at work and church, I don't feel depressed or worried in that way. My main issue is just the confusion and not being able to keep my thoughts and actions in order.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your personality, such as becoming more impulsive, socially inappropriate, or less concerned about how your actions affect others compared to how you were before?\n\nPatient: Yes, my family and pastor have pointed out that I seem \"out of step\" and behave in ways that don't fit the situation at home and church. At work, I'll sometimes start pacing, waving my hands, or making faces without really knowing why. Even when my supervisor tells me to knock it off and get back to work, I struggle to stop.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your sexual behavior, appetite, or your ability to recognize or respond to the emotions of the people around you since these behavioral changes began?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't really noticed any changes in my appetite or sexual behavior. I also don't think I have any trouble recognizing other people's emotions, as those issues aren't really part of what I'm struggling with.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any issues with your memory, such as difficulty remembering recent events, familiar names, or how to perform routine tasks you previously knew well?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think I have trouble remembering names or past events. It's more that I just get really mixed up, lose my train of thought, or lose track of what I'm doing in the moment.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been told by a doctor that you have an underlying medical condition, such as a metabolic issue, a hormonal imbalance, or a neurological disorder that could potentially affect your brain function?\n\nPatient: No, I've never been told that I have any underlying medical conditions, metabolic issues, or neurological disorders. I have always been physically healthy and haven't had any diagnoses like that before.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any difficulty with your speech, such as trouble finding the right words or understanding what others are saying, compared to how you were before these changes started?\n\nPatient: Yes, I've had a really hard time keeping my speech and thoughts on track lately. If someone asks me a simple question, I'll start talking about pallets, but then I'll suddenly jump to fishing with my uncle or a safety video and completely lose my train of thought. By the end of it, I can see on their faces that they are completely confused and have no idea what I was trying to say.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any involuntary muscle twitches, jerky movements, or changes in your gait and coordination, even if they seem minor?\n\nPatient: I haven't really noticed any involuntary muscle twitches or jerky movements. Sometimes I do find myself holding odd poses, pacing, or making faces without realizing why, but it isn't like an involuntary spasm.\n\nDoctor: When you interact with others, do you find it difficult to follow social rules or restrain yourself from saying or doing things that might be considered rude or embarrassing, even when you know you shouldn't?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do have trouble acting in ways that fit the situation, both at home and at church. I sometimes find myself pacing, waving my hands, or making faces without knowing why, even when people tell me to stop. It's not that I'm trying to be rude, but I just can't seem to keep my actions in order or control these behaviors.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any gradual decline in your ability to perform complex daily tasks, such as managing your finances, following a recipe, or navigating familiar routes, compared to how you functioned a year ago?\n\nPatient: Yes, I've noticed a major decline in my ability to plan and carry out tasks over the past year. I used to meticulously plan woodworking projects and fishing trips, but now I can't even stick to basic plans or follow through with things at home or church. Everything at the warehouse just turns into a mess because I can't seem to organize my work anymore.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your physical health recently, such as a decline in your ability to walk steadily, frequent tripping, or any new stiffness in your limbs?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't noticed any stiffness in my limbs, frequent tripping, or trouble walking steadily. Physically, my body seems to move around just fine.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel that your ability to understand complex information or solve problems has become significantly slower or more impaired than it was a year ago?"

But I definitely feel extremely confused lately, and I just can't seem to keep my actions and thoughts organized anymore.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any significant medical illnesses, head injuries, or new medications that coincided with the start of these behavioral changes a year ago?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't had any head injuries or major medical illnesses, and I wasn't taking any new medications back then. I was pretty healthy physically before all of this started happening.\n\nDoctor: Besides the confusion and trouble with tasks, have you been feeling persistently sad, hopeless, or unusually worried and anxious for most of the day over this past year?\n\nPatient: I haven't really been feeling persistently sad, hopeless, or unusually anxious. While I am stressed and frustrated by the trouble I'm having at work and church, I don't feel depressed or worried in that way. My main issue is just the confusion and not being able to keep my thoughts and actions in order.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your personality, such as becoming more impulsive, socially inappropriate, or less concerned about how your actions affect others compared to how you were before?\n\nPatient: Yes, my family and pastor have pointed out that I seem \"out of step\" and behave in ways that don't fit the situation at home and church. At work, I'll sometimes start pacing, waving my hands, or making faces without really knowing why. Even when my supervisor tells me to knock it off and get back to work, I struggle to stop.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your sexual behavior, appetite, or your ability to recognize or respond to the emotions of the people around you since these behavioral changes began?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't really noticed any changes in my appetite or sexual behavior. I also don't think I have any trouble recognizing other people's emotions, as those issues aren't really part of what I'm struggling with.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any issues with your memory, such as difficulty remembering recent events, familiar names, or how to perform routine tasks you previously knew well?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think I have trouble remembering names or past events. It's more that I just get really mixed up, lose my train of thought, or lose track of what I'm doing in the moment.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been told by a doctor that you have an underlying medical condition, such as a metabolic issue, a hormonal imbalance, or a neurological disorder that could potentially affect your brain function?\n\nPatient: No, I've never been told that I have any underlying medical conditions, metabolic issues, or neurological disorders. I have always been physically healthy and haven't had any diagnoses like that before.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any difficulty with your speech, such as trouble finding the right words or understanding what others are saying, compared to how you were before these changes started?\n\nPatient: Yes, I've had a really hard time keeping my speech and thoughts on track lately. If someone asks me a simple question, I'll start talking about pallets, but then I'll suddenly jump to fishing with my uncle or a safety video and completely lose my train of thought. By the end of it, I can see on their faces that they are completely confused and have no idea what I was trying to say.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any involuntary muscle twitches, jerky movements, or changes in your gait and coordination, even if they seem minor?\n\nPatient: I haven't really noticed any involuntary muscle twitches or jerky movements. Sometimes I do find myself holding odd poses, pacing, or making faces without realizing why, but it isn't like an involuntary spasm.\n\nDoctor: When you interact with others, do you find it difficult to follow social rules or restrain yourself from saying or doing things that might be considered rude or embarrassing, even when you know you shouldn't?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do have trouble acting in ways that fit the situation, both at home and at church. I sometimes find myself pacing, waving my hands, or making faces without knowing why, even when people tell me to stop. It's not that I'm trying to be rude, but I just can't seem to keep my actions in order or control these behaviors.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any gradual decline in your ability to perform complex daily tasks, such as managing your finances, following a recipe, or navigating familiar routes, compared to how you functioned a year ago?\n\nPatient: Yes, I've noticed a major decline in my ability to plan and carry out tasks over the past year. I used to meticulously plan woodworking projects and fishing trips, but now I can't even stick to basic plans or follow through with things at home or church. Everything at the warehouse just turns into a mess because I can't seem to organize my work anymore.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your physical health recently, such as a decline in your ability to walk steadily, frequent tripping, or any new stiffness in your limbs?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't noticed any stiffness in my limbs, frequent tripping, or trouble walking steadily. Physically, my body seems to move around just fine.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel that your ability to understand complex information or solve problems has become significantly slower or more impaired than it was a year ago?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure if my actual thinking speed or problem-solving is slower. It's more that I get incredibly mixed up, lose my train of thought, and can't seem to organize my plans or actions anymore.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your sense of smell, or have you had any family members who experienced similar personality changes or movement issues at a relatively young age?"
  }

Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge.  Subtypes: Feeling Keyed Up, Physical Tension, Nervousness\n[S059] Concentration_Difficulty: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank.  Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. (Note: In children, may be expressed by crying, tantrums, freezing, or clinging).  Subtypes: Animal Type, Natural Environment Type, Blood-Injection-Injury Type, Situational Type, Other Type\n[S063] Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Anticipatory Anxiety, Immediate Panic Response, Physiological Panic Symptoms, Freezing\n[S064] Phobic_Avoidance_Endure: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety. Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns.  Subtypes: Mirror Checking or Avoidance, Excessive Camouflaging, Skin Picking, Reassurance Seeking, Comparing, Seeking Cosmetic Procedures\n[S069] Intrusive_Symptoms: Recurrent, involuntary, and intrusive distressing memories of the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.  Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.  Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.  Subtypes: Scanning for Danger, Safety Checking, Sustained Alertness\n[S080] Exaggerated_Startle_Response: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Auditory Startle, Tactile Startle, Visual Startle, Slow Recovery\n[S081] Stress_Related_Maladaptive_Response: Emotional or behavioral symptoms appearing in response to an identifiable stressor.  Subtypes: With Depressed Mood, With Anxiety, With Mixed Anxiety and Depressed Mood, With Disturbance of Conduct, With Mixed Disturbance of Emotions and Conduct, Unspecified\n[S082] Depersonalization_Derealization: Altered sense of the reality of one's self or surroundings after a traumatic event.  Subtypes: Depersonalization, Derealization, Time Distortion\n\n=== PATIENT UTTERANCE ===\nI haven't really been feeling persistently sad, hopeless, or unusually anxious. Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities. Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns.  Subtypes: Mirror Checking or Avoidance, Excessive Camouflaging, Skin Picking, Reassurance Seeking, Comparing, Seeking Cosmetic Procedures\n[S069] Intrusive_Symptoms: Recurrent, involuntary, and intrusive distressing memories of the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.  Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.  Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.  Subtypes: Scanning for Danger, Safety Checking, Sustained Alertness\n[S080] Exaggerated_Startle_Response: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Auditory Startle, Tactile Startle, Visual Startle, Slow Recovery\n[S081] Stress_Related_Maladaptive_Response: Emotional or behavioral symptoms appearing in response to an identifiable stressor.  Subtypes: With Depressed Mood, With Anxiety, With Mixed Anxiety and Depressed Mood, With Disturbance of Conduct, With Mixed Disturbance of Emotions and Conduct, Unspecified\n[S082] Depersonalization_Derealization: Altered sense of the reality of one's self or surroundings after a traumatic event.
